He argues that Goldman was not given the opportunity to explain or deny her alleged statements at the preliminary hearing [and] Goldman was not a witness at trial [and] could not have given further testimony. (See Doc. No. 116 at 336:23-348:15; Doc. No. 128 at 150:6-152:11); Evid. Code §§ 770, 1235.
(i) Goldman’s Preliminary Hearing Testimony was Unconfronted but Admissible
As noted, Goldman died shortly before trial. Her August 1989 preliminary hearing testimony was admitted at trial as testimony of an unavailable witness. (RT 4334.)
It is clear that the Confrontation Clause also does not bar statements introduced for a non-hearsay purpose. See Street, 471 U.S. at 414, 105 S.Ct. 2078 (defendant’s rights under Confrontation Clause not violated by introduction of accomplice’s confession for non-hearsay purpose); see also Gonzales, 2012 WL 6004520, at *16 (the rule of Crawford only applies to testimonial statements that were introduced to establish the truth of the matter asserted); Crawford, 541 U.S. at 59 n. 9, 124 S.Ct. 1354, citing Street, 471 U.S. at 414, 105 S.Ct. 2078 (same). For reasons stated in claim II(L), absent any valid waiver by petitioner, Crawford bars use of Goldman’s unconfronted testimonial hearsay statements, but not use of Goldman’ statements for the non-hearsay purpose of impeaching Goldman’s credibility.
In California, inconsistent statements are admissible to attack the credibility of a declarant even without any giving the de-clarant an opportunity to explain or deny the inconsistent statement. See Gonzales, 2012 WL 6004520, at *13, citing Evid. Code § 1202. The rule of Crawford “only applies to testimonial statements that were introduced to establish the truth of the matter asserted.” Id.; accord, Davis, 547 U.S. at 823, 126 S.Ct. 2266 (Confrontation Clause applies only to testimonial hearsay).
Here, the state supreme court could reasonably have determined Stokes’s testimony was offered to impeach Goldman’s preliminary hearing testimony. As discussed in claim II(L), Goldman’s credibility was a significant issue at trial and was argued by both petitioner and respondent. Both parties invoked Stokes’s testimony in arguing Goldman’s credibility (directly) and Buchanan’s credibility (indirectly). (See e.g., claims II(P), XXVI, XXVIII.) Schultz, as a matter of tactics may have hoped that as a result of Stokes’s testimony the jury would discount Goldman as a witness and discount Buchanan at least to the extent his testimony was consistent with Goldman’s.
In any event, the jury was instructed regarding consideration of Stokes’s testimony including for purposes of determining the believability of Goldman and other trial witnesses. (CT 399—401.)
(ii) No Prejudice
Even if there was trial court error as alleged, the California Supreme Court reasonably could have found such error not prejudicial under Brecht.
A Confrontation Clause violation is harmless and so does not justify habeas relief unless it “had substantial and injurious effect or influence in determining the jury’s verdict.” Brecht, 507 U.S. at 623, 113 S.Ct. 1710. “[W]hen a habeas court is in grave doubt as to the harmlessness of an error that affects substantial rights, it should grant relief.” O’Neal v. McAninch, 513 U.S. 432, 445, 115 S.Ct. 992, 130 L.Ed.2d 947 (1995). In general, the inquiry into whether the constitutionally erroneous introduction of a piece of evidence had a substantial and injurious effect is guided by several factors: “the importance of the testimony, whether the testimony was cumulative, the presence or absence of evidence corroborating or contradicting the testimony, the extent of cross-examination permitted, and the overall strength of the prosecution’s case.” Ocampo, 649 F.3d at 1114.
Here, Stokes’s testimony appears significantly cumulative of and corroborated by Buchanan’s testimony. See e.g., Ocampo, 649 F.3d at 1114 (inquiry into Confrontation Clause error considers whether er-rantly admitted evidence was cumulative). For example, Buchanan testified that on the night of the murders, he saw petitioner take the Venetian blind out of the hall closet and go into the bedroom (RT 4665-66), and then exit the apartment bedroom and return the Venetian blind to the closet; he also saw a kitchen knife in the bedroom (RT 4706-07). See Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 898, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921.
Buchanan also testified that RC and petitioner returned later than night and bought drugs for the roommates with money they did not have earlier that evening and that petitioner later disposed of some of his clothing by throwing it out of Buchanan’s car. (RT 4675-85.) Significantly, petitioner’s admission of his role in the crimes to Goldman and Buchanan and discussion of events surrounding (RT 4352-79; 4606-08, 4687-9494) were consistent in the record and not controverted by Stokes’s testimony of Goldman’s statements.
Petitioner’s admission was corroborated by evidence of RC and petitioner leaving the apartment together the night of the crimes and returning together later that evening with RC acting strangely (id.; see also Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 898, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921) and petitioner initially depressed following the crimes (RT 4352-63).
Goldman’s testimony about petitioner being essentially fearless and aggressive was not directly controverted by Stokes’s testimony (RT 4398) or by Buchanan’s testimony that petitioner “never threatened my life in my presence.” (RT 4745.)
Petitioner’s suggestion that Stokes, by testifying as a police detective effectively vouched for Goldman’s consistent (with the preliminary hearing) statements appears to be merely speculative. Petitioner does not demonstrate on the evidentiary record that Stokes either expressed his personal belief in Goldman’s veracity or suggested information not known to the jury supported Goldman’s statements. See Hermanek, 289 F.3d at 1098; see also McKoy, 771 F.2d at 1210-11 (“The rule that a prosecutor may not express his personal opinion of the defendant’s guilt or his belief in the credibility of witnesses is firmly established.”).
(in) Conclusions
Accordingly, a fair-minded jurist could have found that petitioner failed to establish prejudicial trial court error in admitting Stokes’s testimony of Goldman statements to him.
It does not appear that the California Supreme Court’s rejection of the claim was contrary to, or an unreasonable application of, clearly established federal law, as determined by the Supreme Court, or that the state court’s ruling was based on an unreasonable determination of the facts in light of the evidence presented in the state court proceeding. See 28 U.S.C. § 2254(d).
Claim XXI is denied.
C. Prosecutorial Misconduct Claims
1. Clearly Established Law
A petitioner is entitled to habeas corpus relief if the prosecutor’s misconduct “so infected the trial with unfairness as to make the resulting conviction a denial of due process.” Donnelly v. DeChristoforo, 416 U.S. 637, 643, 94 S.Ct. 1868, 40 L.Ed.2d 431 (1974).
To constitute a due process violation, the prosecutorial misconduct must be “of sufficient significance to result in the denial of the defendant’s right to a fair trial.” Greer v. Miller, 483 U.S. 756, 765, 107 S.Ct. 3102, 97 L.Ed.2d 618 (1987), quoting United States v. Bagley, 473 U.S. 667, 682, 105 S.Ct. 3375, 87 L.Ed.2d 481 (1985). “Before a federal court may overturn a conviction resulting from a state trial ... it must be established not merely that the [State’s action] is undesirable, erroneous, or even universally condemned, but that it violated some right which was guaranteed to the defendant by the Fourteenth Amendment.” Smith v. Phillips, 455 U.S. 209, 221, 102 S.Ct. 940, 71 L.Ed.2d 78 (1982), quoting Cupp, 414 U.S. at 146, 94 S.Ct. 396.
Any claim of prosecutorial misconduct must be reviewed within the context of the entire trial. Greer, 483 U.S. at 765-66, 107 S.Ct. 3102; United States v. Weitzenhoff, 35 F.3d 1275, 1291 (9th Cir. 1994). The court must keep in mind that “[t]he touchstone of due process analysis in cases of alleged prosecutorial misconduct is the fairness of the trial, not the culpability of the prosecutor” and “the aim of due process is not punishment of society for the misdeeds of the prosecutor but avoidance of an unfair trial to the accused.” Phillips, 455 U.S. at 219, 102 S.Ct. 940. “Improper argument does not, per se, violate a defendant’s constitutional rights.” Thompson v. Borg, 74 F.3d 1571, 1576 (9th Cir. 1996), quoting Jeffries v. Blodgett, 5 F.3d 1180, 1191 (9th Cir. 1993).
Furthermore, the Supreme Court has stated:
[Arguments of counsel generally carry less weight with a jury than do instructions from the court. The former are usually billed in advance to the jury as matters of argument, not evidence ... and are likely viewed as the statements of advocates; the latter, we have often recognized, are viewed as definitive and binding statements of the law. Arguments of counsel which misstate the law are subject to objection and to correction by the court. This is not to say that prosecutorial misrepresentations may never have a decisive effect on the jury, but only that they are not to be judged as having the same force as an instruction from the court. And the arguments of counsel, like the instructions of the court, must be judged in the context in which they are made.
Boyde, 494 U.S. at 384-85, 110 S.Ct. 1190.
If prosecutorial misconduct is established, and it was constitutional error, the error must be evaluated pursuant to the harmless error test set forth in Brecht. See Thompson, 74 F.3d at 1577 (“Only if the argument were constitutional error would we have to decide whether the constitutional error was harmless.”). A petitioner is entitled to relief in this context only where the constitutional violations exerted a “substantial and injurious” effect on the judgment. Brecht, 507 U.S. at 620, 113 S.Ct. 1710; Fields v. Woodford, 309 F.3d 1095, 1109 (9th Cir. 2002) (stating the Ninth Circuit applies Brecht if misconduct of constitutional dimension is established), amended 315 F.3d 1062 (9th Cir. 2002).
2. Review of claim XIII
Petitioner alleges prosecutorial misconduct by failing to disclose evidence under Brady v. Maryland and by knowingly presenting false and misleading evidence under Napue v. Illinois, violating his rights under the Fifth, Sixth, Eighth and Fourteenth Amendments to the U.S. Constitution. (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶¶ 722-768.)
a. State Court Direct and Collateral Review
The California Supreme Court summarily denied all these allegations as presented in petitioner’s state habeas petition. (Lod. Doc. No. 10.)
That court also considered and rejected certain of these allegations on the merits on direct appeal, as discussed, post.
b. Analysis of False Testimony Allegations
Petitioner alleges the prosecutor knowingly allowed witnesses to testify falsely.
(i) Clearly Established Law — False Testimony
The knowing use of false or perjured testimony against a defendant to obtain a conviction is unconstitutional. Napue v. Illinois, 360 U.S. 264, 269, 79 S.Ct. 1173, 3 L.Ed.2d 1217 (1959) (prosecutor’s knowing presentation of false testimony violates due process); see also Mooney v. Holohan, 294 U.S. 103, 112, 55 S.Ct. 340, 79 L.Ed. 791 (1935) (conviction contrived by prosecution’s deliberate presentation of perjured testimony violated due process).
In Napue, the Supreme Court held that the knowing use of false testimony to obtain a conviction violates due process regardless of whether the prosecutor solicited the false testimony or merely allowed it to go uncorrected when it appeared. 360 U.S. at 269, 79 S.Ct. 1173. The Court explained that the principle that a state may not knowingly use false testimony to obtain a conviction — even false testimony that goes only to the credibility of the witness — is “implicit in any concept of ordered liberty.” Id.
A conviction obtained by the knowing use of perjured testimony “must be set aside if there is any reasonable likelihood that the false testimony could have affected the judgment of the jury.” Bagley, 473 U.S. at 678, 105 S.Ct. 3375. Nevertheless, simple inconsistencies in testimony are insufficient to establish that a prosecutor knowingly permitted the admission of false testimony. United States v. Zuno-Arce, 44 F.3d 1420, 1423 (9th Cir. 1995). “Discrepancies in ... testimony ... could as easily flow from errors in recollection as from lies.” Id.
To warrant habeas relief, Petitioner must establish that: 1) the testimony was actually false; 2) the prosecution knew or should have known it to be false; and 3) there is a reasonable likelihood that the false testimony could have affected the jury’s verdict. Tayborn, 251 F.3d at 1130.
(ii) False Evidence — Room and Board, Personal Loans and Trial Preparation
Petitioner revisits allegation discussed in claim 11(E), and alleges that the prosecution allowed false testimony by Buchanan that might reflect on his credibility — testimony regarding the favorable room and board agreement with Ms. Strickland facilitated by the prosecution including alleged personal loans to Buchanan thereunder. He also alleges the prosecution downplayed the extent of its preparation of Buchanan for trial. (Doc. No. 116 at 28:21-29:2.)
Petitioner points to Buchanan’s allegedly false testimony that “he did not receive a single thing” from the prosecution team (RT 4766); that he had been working for Strickland at her boarding house and did not owe her anything and that she owed him $25,000 (RT 4716); and that he met with' prosecutor Hahus only a couple times before trial. (RT 4766.)
Room and Board
Petitioner alleges Buchanan testified falsely about the “[room and board] deal, brokered by the District Attorney’s Office, between Buchanan and [boarding house operator] Ms. Strickland” under which Buchanan’s room and board payments were deferred and payable from the anticipated private reward monies. (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶¶ 735-39; RT 5268-81, 5309-29; SHOP, Ex.’s G, H; see also Brady allegations, post.) He alleges that prosecution investigator William Martin arranged the deal and memorialized the agreement on Fresno County District Attorney letterhead, for “long-term and (room and board) [on a] deferred basis.” (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶ 736; RT 5276; CT 583.) The memorializing document was signed by Buchanan and witnessed by Martin and another prosecution investigator in order to “protect Ms. Strickland because [Martin] did not trust Buchanan to fulfill his obligations to her” (RT 5268-5282; see CT 582-83), and to ensure Buchanan could be located when needed to testify, (id.).
Under this deal, petitioner alleges Buchanan received room and board in exchange for a promise to pay $350 per month out of the $5,000 private reward he expected to receive for helping to convict petitioner. (RT 5276; SHCP, Ex. G; Doc. No. 116 at 44:11-16.)
Petitioner alleges that by the end of trial 24 months later, Buchanan’s debt to Strickland had risen to over $9100 covering room, board and personal loans. (Doc. No. 116 at 44:18.) Strickland herself placed the final- debt at a little over $5000 and ultimately reduced it to the $3,500 paid by Buchanan, leaving $1,500 of the private reward money for Buchanan to keep. (RT 5325-28.) The record reflects that Strickland wrote down the debt because she felt sorry for Buchanan and to help his family. (Id.)
The trial court, on motion for new trial rejected petitioner’s claim that the foregoing constituted prejudicial prosecutorial misconduct. (CT 541-47; RT 1/17/92 at 9-17.) That court viewed the Strickland deal as “a [prosecution] favor.” (RT 5212.) Prosecutor Hahus agreed that “[w]ell, sure it’s something, yeah, absolutely, absolutely.” (Id.) But ultimately, the trial court concluded that:
Although there was assistance given to Mr. Buchanan, it’s true that the District Attorney’s Office did not buy him anything. He did receive something of value, and I have to agree to that, having a place to stay for who knows how long is obviously valuable. It’s valuable if it’s just one day. It’s certainly more valuable as time goes on. And Mr. Buchanan benefitted by that. But I do have to find that they only acted as a conduit for Mr. Buchanan, that Mrs. Strickland [the landlady] could have thrown him out anytime she wanted to. The fact that she did not was not something that I can find the District Attorney’s Office or the Police Department was somehow involved in.
(RT, Jan. 17, 1992, 16-17.)
Similarly, the California Supreme Court on direct appeal considered these same allegations and found the noted false testimony to be harmless error, stating that:
We conclude the prosecutor did knowingly fail to correct a false impression— indeed, knowingly exploited the false impression in his argument to the jury— that the prosecution had not done Buchanan any favors that might reflect on his credibility.
The impression was false because a prosecution investigator facilitated an arrangement with the proprietor of a boarding house under which Buchanan received room and board in return for IOU’s backed by the reward money he was hoping to receive. However, we conclude that, in light of other information the jury had about Buchanan’s arrangement with the proprietor of the boarding house, as well as other indications of his interest in obtaining the reward, the prosecutor’s action was harmless beyond a reasonable doubt.
On cross-examination, Buchanan was asked about his contacts with Melvin King, a defense investigator. Buchanan said, among other things, that King bought him lunch, as well as a beer, and “wanted to take me to church and wanted to take me skydiving and stuff like that.”
On redirect, the prosecutor asked Buchanan how many times he had spoken to defense investigator King. Buchanan responded he had done so perhaps a dozen times, in person or on the phone. The prosecutor asked him- what King had bought him. Buchanan said King had bought him lunch, beer on three occasions, and a pair of shoes.
The prosecutor and Buchanan then engaged in the following colloquy. “Q. At any time have you spoken with anybody who’s told you they were from my office, from the D.A.’s office? [¶] A. No, sir, only when they’ve come to pick me up for court. [¶] Q. You’ve talked to me a couple of times; is that right? [¶] A. Yes, sir. [¶] Q. At any time have the folks who’ve come to pick you up from my office or me, have we bought you anything? [¶] A. Not a single thing, sir.” In his argument to the jury, the prosecutor sought to exploit the false impression he had created — that unlike the defense, the prosecution had done nothing for Buchanan that might reflect on his credibility. “His statements to Mel King, the only investigator who supplied him with money or alcohol or food was never to back off of his statement that [defendant] made that confession. Now, he’s con-wise. ‘This guy has taken me out to lunch. This guy is taking me out buying me beer, why kill the goose that’s laying the golden egg? You want to hear something, pal, say whatever you want to hear, let’s have another Colt 45.’ But did he ever, ever back out of that statement?”
As previously stated, the impression was false because a prosecution investigator facilitated an arrangement with the proprietor of a boarding house under which Buchanan received room and board in return for IOU’s, amounting to $3,000 or $4,000, backed by the reward money he was hoping to receive. The prosecution investigator even went so far as to draft a document signed by Buchanan under which he agreed the district attorney’s office had his permission to discharge his IOU’s before he received the remainder of the reward. The prosecutor was aware of this arrangement; he so testified at the hearing on the motion for a new trial.
That said, we conclude there is no reasonable likelihood the false impression created by Buchanan’s testimony could have affected the judgment of the jury. While the jury did not know of the role the prosecution investigator played in facilitating the agreement between Buchanan and the proprietor of the boarding house, the jury knew, through Buchanan’s testimony and that of the defense investigator, of the agreement itself. They knew, through the defense investigator’s testimony, that Buchanan has said on “many” occasions “[h]e was expecting to receive the reward, deduct what money he owed his landlord, buy himself a pickup truck or a car and leave town and start his life over.” Finally, they knew through Goldman’s testimony that Buchanan had told her “he was going to turn in [defendant] so he could get the reward.”
Defendant contends his trial counsel was ineffective in failing to impeach Buchanan with the room and board agreement backed by his IOU’s, as well as with the role of the prosecutor’s investigator in facilitating the arrangement. However, for the reasons stated, it is not reasonably probable that, but for counsel’s inaction, the result of the trial would have been different. [Citation]
-FOOTNOTE-
n.10 Defense counsel knew of the arrangement.
- END FOOTNOTE
Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 909-11, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921.
The state supreme court’s rejection of these allegations for the reasons stated by that court was not unreasonable. The record suggests Buchanan requested assistance from investigator Martin because he had nowhere to stay upon his noted March 1989 OR release from jail and pending his testimony against petitioner. (SHOP, Ex. H.) While the jury may not have known the extent to which the prosecutor’s office helped arrange the agreement between Buchanan and Strickland, the jury was aware of the agreement and its terms and conditions. {See e.g., RT 4387, 4857.)
Investigator Martin testified at the hearing on motion for new trial that this type of agreement was not unusual. (RT 5293-97.) He testified that he told both Buchanan and Strickland that issues relating to Buchanan’s payment obligation were totally between them. (RT 5275.) He testified that notwithstanding Strickland’s unsolicited mailing of Buchanan’s account statements (bills) to the District Attorney’s office, (RT 5288-90, 5309), the parties (Buchanan and Strickland) intended that Buchanan’s promise to pay was limited to any reward proceeds that might become available and that no public funds would be used to pay the debt. (RT 5280-82, 5305-06.) Strickland acknowledged as much. (RT 5321-23.) Strickland testified she did not send the bills to the prosecution with the expectation that the latter was responsible for paying or would pay them. (Id.)
Strickland testified that she sometimes took in boarders on an IOU basis. (Id.) That she may have periodically phoned Hahus’s office asking when she would get paid (see e.g., RT 5289, 5321-26, 5326, 5404-06) does not alone demonstrate public funds backstopped the agreement. Moreover, Hahus confirmed to Strickland that the private reward was contingent upon conviction and Buchanan’s entitlement to the reward. (RT 5404-06.)
Petitioner fails to support with evidence his allegation that the prosecution was financially liable under the Buchanan-Strickland agreement. Petitioner argues, but does not support in the evidence that the prosecution provided $9,100 in benefits and/or participated with Strickland in her decision to write-down Buchanan’s debt, (Doc. No. 116 at 74:28-75:2), and/or that Strickland offered benefits to Buchanan as an agent of the prosecution, (see id. at 98:11-27).
To the contrary, prosecutor Hahus testified at the new trial motion that he was unaware of any relationship or dealing between Buchanan and Strickland apart from the noted agreement facilitated by Martin that was reimbursable from the private reward money. (RT 5449-50; CT 582-583; SHOP, Ex.’s G, H.) Hahus noted that the room and board agreement with Strickland was in place when he as assigned to the case. (RT 5394.) Hahus also testified at the motion for new trial that the only thing he bought Buchanan was lunch once while Buchanan was testifying (RT 5408-09); and that Buchanan refused anything else for fear it would appear the prosecution was trying to buy his testimony (RT 5409-10).
In sum, the documentation prepared by investigator Martin memorializing the Striekland-Buehanan arrangement and the noted testimony of Martin, Hahus and Strickland demonstrate the prosecution was not financially responsible under the Buchanan-Strickland agreement and the prosecution was unaware of any dispute between Strickland and Buchanan regarding sums owing between them and/or write-down thereof.
Personal Loans
Petitioner alleges that Buchanan received personal loans from Strickland of at least $760. (Doc. No. 116 at 44:18-23, 98:20-22; RT 5321; SHOP, Ex.’s. H, I.) Petitioner points to line item loan amounts included in the above noted bills Strickland sent to the prosecution, (see SHOP, Ex. I), and suggests these loans were an undisclosed inducement for Buchanan’s testimony given that Buchanan was “a key [prosecution] witness.” (RT 5271, 5390.)
The record reflects that during the time the agreement was in effect, Strickland sent to the Fresno District Attorney’s Office copies of Buchanan’s IOU’s that included line itemed loans. (SHOP, Ex. I.) Nonetheless, this allegation fails for the same reasons the immediately above room and board allegations fail. At bottom, petitioner has not demonstrated on the eviden-tiary record that prosecution funds were loaned or paid to either Strickland or Buchanan for purposes of the alleged Strickland loans, or otherwise.
Trial Preparation
Petitioner alleges Buchanan testified falsely about the number of times prosecutor Hahus met with him prior to trial. Petitioner argues the falsely reduced number of meetings Hahus had with Buchanan was prejudicial to the extent it caused Buchanan to appear uncoached and more credible. (See Doc. No. 116 at 29:6-10, 47:19-23, 55:6-10, 62:1-12, 64:19-23.)
As noted, Buchanan testified that he had met with Hahus “a couple of times.” (RT 4766.) Hahus later admitted at the motion for new trial hearing that he had “met” with Buchanan approximately 12 times pri- or to trial, had spoken to Buchanan by phone at least once, and his investigators had contacted Buchanan several times. (RT 5390; SHOP, Ex. N at 1-3.) Notably, Hahus in his closing argument explaining discrepancies between Buchanan’s testimony at preliminary hearing and at trial, stated that “it’s pretty clear Buchanan was not prepared to testify, that he was not scripted to testify.” (RT 4968.)
As an initial matter, the state supreme court reasonably could have rejected respondent’s argument that Hahus was speaking in merely colloquial terms — such that there was no false impression or that any impression as to the number of meetings was neither false nor material. (-See Doc. No. 125 at 138:24-140:24.) The record reflects that Buchanan significantly understated the number of pretrial meetings he had with Buchanan.
Nevertheless, the state supreme court reasonably could have denied this allegation as harmless error. {See e.g., RT 4969.) Petitioner has not demonstrated on the evidentiary record that additional trial preparation would cause jurors to discount the credibility of a witness. Moreover, the jury was well aware of the facts relating to Buchanan’s credibility including the testimony and statements of Goldman. {See claim(s) 11(E), II(P), XXVI, XXVIII.)
The state supreme court concluded in these regards that:
The jury knew Goldman and Buchanan were drug addicts, that they had been using drugs almost daily around the time of defendant’s statements, and that Buchanan had used drugs the day before his testimony. It knew that, even though he had initially denied it, Buchanan was aware of the $5,000 reward, that Buchanan had told others he wanted the reward for turning defendant in, and that he had then testified the reward was not the reason he had come forward. It knew room and board expenses incurred by Buchanan were to be deducted from the reward. [Citation] It knew Buchanan disliked defendant, and that he wanted revenge against defendant for tearing up a picture of his daughter.
Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 907 n.8, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921. A fair-minded jurist could have concluded that these same facts would be related to the need to prepare Buchanan for trial.
Harmless Error
For the reasons stated, it was not unreasonable for the state supreme court to find pursuant to Napue that the foregoing alleged false testimony was harmless rather than material error. 360 U.S. at 269, 79 S.Ct. 1173; see also Dow v. Virga, 729 F.3d 1041, 1047 (9th Cir. 2013), quoting Giglio v. United States, 405 U.S. 150, 154, 92 S.Ct. 763, 31 L.Ed.2d 104 (1972) (a new trial is required if “the false testimony could ... in any reasonable likelihood have affected the judgment of the jury....”).
The jury was aware of the Strickland-Buchanan room and board agreement and the entirely private reward funding that backed it. They were aware of Buchanan’s desire to get the reward, pay his bill to Strickland and leave town. They were aware Buchanan’s alleged grudge against petitioner. The jury was aware of other noted facts relating to Buchanan’s credibility; his observations on the night of the murders and his statements regarding petitioner’s admission of involvement in the murders.
The jury was aware that Buchanan had met with Hahus, the prosecution team and the defense team. They were instructed that the argument of counsel, including Hahus’s noted closing argument that defense investigators had made several apparently minor purchases for Buchanan and that the prosecution had not purchased “a single thing” for him, was not evidence.
For the reasons stated the state supreme court could have found no reasonable likelihood the noted alleged false testimony could have affected the judgment of the jury. Napue, 360 U.S. at 269, 79 S.Ct. 1173; see also Dow, 729 F.3d at 1047 (a new trial is required if “the false testimony could ... in any reasonable likelihood have affected the judgment of the jury....”) That same court reasonably could have found that considering the record as a whole, the alleged false testimony did not “falsely buttress” Buchanan’s credibility. Cf., Hayes v. Brown, 399 F.3d 972, 986 (9th Cir. 2005) (reversing conviction under Napue because concealment of a leniency deal allowed the State to “falsely buttress[]” credibility of a key witness). Petitioner was not denied a fair trial for these same reasons.
(in) False Evidence — Penal Code Section 128
Petitioner revisits allegations in claims II(K) (alleging ineffective assistance of counsel) and VIII (alleging trial court error), alleging here that the prosecutor engaged in misconduct, bolstering Buchanan’s noted alleged false testimony by making him aware of the potential for perjury provided by Penal Code section 128. (Doc. No. 116 at 40:3-13; RT 4764-66.)
Petitioner argues that Buchanan’s bolstered testimony was false for the same reasons argued in claims II(K) and VIII, i.e., that section 128 is not a meaningful deterrent to perjury and the chances of a successful prosecution under section 128 are so remote as to be irrelevant. As discussed in those claims, the state supreme court rejected this reasoning on direct appeal. See, Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 912, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921.
Respondent contends these allegations are unexhausted (Doc. No. 125 at 146:18-147:3) and lack support in the record (id.). The court does not reach respondent’s exhaustion argument for the reasons stated in claims II(K) and VIII and because the claim is denied on the merits.
As noted, the prosecutor was allowed to elicit testimony from Buchanan that the prosecutor had made him aware of Penal Code section 128, which provided that:
Every person who, by willful perjury or subornation of perjury, procures the conviction and execution of any innocent person, is punishable by death or life imprisonment without possibility of parole. The penalty shall be determined pursuant to Sections 190.3 and 190.4.
Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 911, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921.
To summarize the discussion in claims II(K) and VIII, the California Supreme Court considered and rejected on direct appeal the allegation that prosecutor Ha-hus improperly asked Buchanan whether he had been made aware of section 128, as follows:
Defendant argues section 128 is not a meaningful deterrent to perjury. “Given the inevitable time lapse between potential conviction and execution, and the remote prospect of conclusive exonerating evidence being discovered after an execution, the chances of a successful prosecution under Penal Code section 128 in any case are so remote as to make the proffered' evidence wholly irrelevant.”
We decline to second-guess the Legislature, which in enacting the section, clearly believed it would have a meaningful deterrent effect. Admittedly, the delay between conviction and execution has grown exponentially since section 128 was enacted in 1872. However, by 1997, when the Legislature amended and thereby reaffirmed the section, the notion of swift justice in capital cases was already a thing of the past. Moreover, with the advent of DNA testing, “the prospect of conclusive exonerating evidence being discovered after an execution” is, if anything, less “remote.” In any event, 'the stakes under section 128, “death or life imprisonment without possibility of parole,” are so high a potential perjurer may well decide it is not worth the risk, however small.
-FOOTNOTE-
n.ll The 1977 amendment added the phrase “or life imprisonment without possibility of parole,” as well as the second sentence. (Stats. 1977, ch. 316, § 3, p. 1256, eff. Aug.ll, 1977.)
- END FOOTNOTE
Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 912, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921.
For the reasons stated, the state supreme court could have found that considering the record as a whole, the alleged false testimony did not “falsely buttress” Buchanan’s credibility, such that there was no reasonable likelihood the noted alleged false testimony could have affected the judgment of the jury. Napue, 360 U.S. at 269, 79 S.Ct. 1173; cf., Hayes, 399 F.3d at 986. Petitioner was not denied a fair trial because the evidence could be seen as proper.
(iv) False Evidence — Stokes Testimony of Goldman’s Statements
Finally, petitioner largely revisits allegations in claim II(L), (alleging ineffective assistance of counsel), that detective Stokes testified falsely when he described Goldman’s statements to him regarding the actions and conversations of RC and petitioner on the day of the murders including as to a knife used the in murders (RT 4397). (Doc. No. 116 at 31:1-9.) Here the allegations are framed as prosecutorial misconduct in presenting false evidence relating the following allegedly inconsistent testimony.
Goldman testified at the preliminary hearing that:
• She saw petitioner and RC leave the apartment right around the day of the Presidential election. (RT 4339-40.)
• Before they left “[t]he only thing [petitioner] did was walk into the kitchen and he looked in the drawer. That’s all he did.” (RT 4343.)
• She saw RC open the hallway closet, and “get a cord.” (RT 4345.)
• She saw that a cord on one of the Venetian blinds in the apartment’s bedroom, not the hallway closet, was gone. (RT 4347.)
• She did not know how long [petitioner and RC] were gone. (RT 4349.)
• When they returned, RC packed his bags and left. (RT 4350.)
• Petitioner admitted to her that he was present when RC killed his grandmother and Louis Freiri, but that “Gail, I swear I had nothing to do with this.” (RT 4357.)
• Petitioner made the “general statement” that “he wasn’t afraid of anyone saying anything about him, because if they did, they wouldn’t do it again.” (RT 4375.)
• After petitioner found out RC and Catón had died, “he wasn’t as depressed as he had been before.” (RT 4378.)
At trial, the prosecutor elicited testimony from detective Stokes (see RT 4590-4616) that Goldman told him:
• She saw petitioner remove a knife from a drawer in the apartment kitchen before he and RC left the apartment on the night of the crimes. (RT 4602.)
• Two days later petitioner “asked her to go into the bedroom with him, he want — he wanted to tell her something.” (RT 4606.)
• Buchanan followed them in (RT 4607) and petitioner stated “he had been involved in something bad.” (RT 4606.)
• “[Petitioner] had said that he would kill her if she or anyone else talked about what he had told them.” (RT 4615.)
• After the deaths of Ms. Catón and RC petitioner “became aggressive toward her and Buchanan, and made statements regarding the fact that if they revealed what he had told them that they would be hurt and killed, as would anyone else.” (RT 4616.)
• Goldman told him about “a Venetian blind that had been in the hall closet that was removed from the hall closet the night of the homicide, and that that Venetian blind was — taken by [petitioner] ... into a bedroom and that the cord was removed from that Venetian blind and then the Venetian blind was placed back inside the hall closet.” (RT 4534.)
The state supreme court would not have been unreasonable in denying these allegations consistent with the discussion in' claim II(L). Stokes’s testimony could reasonably be seen as impeaching Goldman’s preliminary hearing testimony, a non-hearsay purpose.
Petitioner’s contention that Stokes coerced Goldman’s statements lacks support in the record. Stokes denied that at the time of the interrogation he threatened to arrest Goldman if she would not talk with him. (RT 4595-97.) Goldman stated “Detective Stokes, I’m sorry, I lied to you. I’m sorry I lied to you. I want to tell you the whole truth right now.” (Id.) The state supreme court reasonably could have found Goldman’s statements to Stokes during the interrogation were voluntary.
Furthermore, it was not unreasonable for the state supreme court to find, under Napue, and for all the reasons stated above that foregoing alleged false testimony was in any event harmless rather than material error. 360 U.S. at 269, 79 S.Ct. 1173; see also Dow, 729 F.3d at 1047 (a new trial is required if “the false testimony could ... in any reasonable likelihood have affected the judgment of the jury....”).
Stokes’s testimony could be seen as substantially cumulative of Buchanan’s testimony. Cf., Ocampo, 649 F.3d at 1114 (inquiry into Confrontation Clause error considers whether errantly admitted evidence was cumulative). For example, Buchanan testified that on the night of the murders, he saw petitioner take the Venetian blind out of the hall closet and go into the bedroom (RT 4665-66), and then exit the bedroom and return the Venetian blind to the closet; he also saw a kitchen knife in the bedroom (RT 4706-07). See also Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 898, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921. Buchanan testified that RC and petitioner returned later than night; bought drugs for the roommates with money they did not have earlier that evening; and petitioner disposed of some clothing by tossing it out of Buchanan’s car. (RT 4682-85.)
Significantly, petitioner’s admission of his role in the crimes to Goldman and Buchanan and discussion of events surrounding (RT 4352-79; 4606-08, 4687-94) were consistent in the record and not controverted by Stokes’s testimony of Goldman’s statements. Furthermore, petitioner’s admission was corroborated by Goldman’s consistent testimony that RC and petitioner left the apartment together the night of the crimes and returned together later that evening whereupon RC acted strangely; (RT 4349-79); see also Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 898, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921; and by petitioner’s initial depression following the crimes (RT 4352-54, 4378). Goldman’s testimony about petitioner being essentially fearless and aggressive also was not directly controverted by Stokes’s testimony (RT 4398) or by Buchanan’s testimony that petitioner “never threatened my life in my presence.” (RT 4745.)
Petitioner, in this claim XIII also alleges that Stokes testified falsely during direct examination about certain of Goldman’s statements contained in his investigative report. (Doc. No. 116 at 31, n.4; RT 4621-22.) However, it appears that Stokes corrected such misstatements during cross-examination. (Id.) The state supreme court reasonably could have found no reasonable likelihood these misstatements of Stokes, corrected on the record could have affected the jury’s verdict. Tayborn, 251 F.3d at 1130. For these reasons, that court would not have been unreasonable in viewing such misstatements as mere inconsistency or failure of Stokes to recollect what was in his investigation reports, rather than false testimony. See Zuno-Arce, 44 F.3d at 1423.
For the reasons stated, the state supreme court could have found that considering the record as a whole, the alleged false testimony did not “falsely buttress” Buchanan’s credibility, such that there was no reasonable likelihood the noted alleged false testimony could have affected the judgment of the jury. Napue, 360 U.S. at 269, 79 S.Ct. 1173; cf., Hayes, 399 F.3d at 986. Petitioner was not denied a fair trial for these same reasons.
c. Analysis of Failure to Disclose Allegations
Petitioner alleges the prosecutor knowingly failed to disclose material evidence to which the defense was entitled.
(i) Clearly Established Law — Failure to Disclose
The Supreme Court has held that if the state fails to disclose exculpatory evidence in violation of Brady v. Maryland, the conviction cannot stand if there is a reasonable probability that the evidence, considered cumulatively, would have produced a different result at trial. 373 U.S. 83, 83 S.Ct. 1194, 10 L.Ed.2d 215 (1963); Kyles v. Whitley, 514 U.S. 419, 434, 115 S.Ct. 1555, 131 L.Ed.2d 490 (1995) (same). If a habeas petitioner establishes the “reasonable probability” of a different result, the error cannot subsequently be found harmless. Id. at 436, 115 S.Ct. 1555. In Brady, the Supreme Court held that the suppression by the prosecution of evidence favorable to an accused violates due process where the evidence is material either to guilt or to punishment, irrespective of the good faith or bad faith of the prosecution. Brady, 373 U.S. at 87, 83 S.Ct. 1194. There are three components to a Brady violation: i) the evidence at issue must be favorable to the accused either because it is exculpatory or because it is impeaching; ii) the evidence must have been suppressed by the State either willfully or inadvertently; and iii) prejudice must have ensued. Banks v. Dretke, 540 U.S. 668, 691, 124 S.Ct. 1256, 157 L.Ed.2d 1166 (2004); Strickler v. Greene, 527 U.S. 263, 281-82, 119 S.Ct. 1936, 144 L.Ed.2d 286 (1999). “Such evidence is material if there is a reasonable probability that, had the evidence been disclosed to the defense, the result of the proceeding would have been different.” Strickler, 527 U.S. at 280, 119 S.Ct. 1936, quoting Bagley, 473 U.S. at 682, 105 S.Ct. 3375. “[T]here is never a real Brady violation unless the nondisclosure was so serious that there is a reasonable probability that the suppressed evidence would have produced a different verdict.” Strickler, 527 U.S. at 281, 119 S.Ct. 1936.
(ii) Analysis of Failure to Disclose Allegations
Petitioner alleges the prosecution failed to disclose to the defense legally discoverable evidence regarding inducements to and impeachment of witness Buchanan to which the defense was entitled, discussed below. (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶ 758-768; see also SHCP, Ex.’s G-K, M, N.)
As a preliminary matter, petitioner argues that pursuant to United States v. Agurs, 427 U.S. 97, 112, 96 S.Ct. 2392, 49 L.Ed.2d 342 (1976), the Napue/Mooney prejudice standard should apply when determining Brady materiality. However, the court conducts a cumulative Brady/Napue prejudice analysis, post. See Kyles, 514 U.S. at 434-37, 115 S.Ct. 1555. Agurs is not authority otherwise.
Contacts between Prosecutor Hahus and Buchanan
Petitioner revisits the Napue allegations above that prosecutor Hahus failed to disclose the extent of his pre-trial contacts with Buchanan — that Hahus met with Buchanan twelve times, not the two times to which Buchanan testified. He also alleges that Hahus had spoken to Buchanan by phone at least once and that prosecution investigators had contacted Buchanan several times.
Petitioner again argues the failure to disclose these contacts made Buchanan’s testimony appear credible, unrehearsed and not impaired by his admitted use of street drugs. He argues that in fact the higher (actual) number of meetings was necessary to prepare Buchanan’s testimony.
However, for the same reasons noted in the above Napue discussion, the state supreme court could reasonably have rejected these allegations. In sum, the jury was aware of the facts relating to Buchanan’s credibility including testimony and statements of Goldman. {See claim(s) 11(E), II(P), XXVI, XXVIII.) The state supreme court concluded in these regards that:
The jury knew Goldman and Buchanan were drug addicts, that they had been using drugs almost daily around the time of defendant’s statements, and that Buchanan had used drugs the day before his .testimony. It knew that, even though he had initially denied it, Buchanan was aware of the $5,000 reward, that Buchanan had told others he wanted the reward for turning defendant in, and that he had then testified the reward was not the reason he had come forward. It knew room and board expenses incurred by Buchanan were to be deducted from the reward. [Citation] It knew Buchanan disliked defendant, and that he wanted revenge against defendant for tearing up a picture of his daughter.
Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 907 n.8, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921. A fair-minded jurist could have concluded that these same facts would be related to the need to prepare Buchanan for trial. Also, the jury was instructed on weighing evidence going toward credibility,, including that:
[In] determining the believability of [Buchanan] you may consider anything that has a tendency in reason to prove or disprove [his] truthfulness ...” [and] ... [e]vidence of the existence or nonexistence of any fact he testified to ...”
(CT 400.)
For the reasons stated the state supreme court could reasonably have found that had the prosecutor disclosed the full extent of contacts he and his office had with Buchanan, there would not have been a reasonable probability of a different outcome.
The Strickland-Buchanan Agreement
Petitioner alleges that prosecutor Hahus did not provide the defense with copies of the noted document memorializing the boarding house agreement between Buchanan and Strickland and bills thereunder. Such evidence, he claims would have impeached Buchanan’s credibility. He alleges that in response to a discovery request by Schultz for “[t]he benefits to be received from the reward, including total monetary and/or any other benefit” (CT 260), the prosecution failed to disclose information by “narrowly limit[ing] its response to the private $5,000 reward.” (CT 288-89.) He alleges that in response to a further discovery request for related documents the prosecution “failed to disclose any documentary evidence of the agreement to use the reward to secure housing and food, the cost of same, the eleven or more documented cash loans, the regular invoicing of the amounts owed and debts incurred, and the ultimate discounts provided by the boardinghouse.” (Doc. No. 116 at 75:19-23, citing SHCP, Ex.’s G, H, I.)
The trial court in denying the motion for new trial and the state supreme court in denying these allegations on direct appeal each found the prosecution’s involvement in the Strickland-Buchanan agreement potentially impeaching. (See RT 1/17/92 at 16-17); Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 910, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921. Nevertheless, the state supreme court was not unreasonable in finding no prejudice, that there was “no reasonable likelihood the false impression created by Buchanan’s testimony [relating to the Strickland room and board agreement] could have affected the judgment of the jury ... [T]he jury knew, through Buchanan’s testimony and that of the defense investigator, of the agreement itself.” Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 910, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921.
Any argument by petitioner that the agreement and bills thereunder constituted evidence of “conflicting claims from [Ms.] Strickland” and on that basis Brady evidence (see Doc. No. 51-1 ¶ 754) fails because the prosecution had no responsibility under the agreement for the reasons stated above. This was clear from prosecution investigator Martin’s memorialization of and report regarding the Strickland-Buchanan agreement (RT 5278-79; SHCP, Ex.’s G, H) and the related testimony of Martin (RT 5280, 5308), Strickland (RT 5323) and Hahus (RT 5405). Petitioner fails to demonstrate on the evidence how benefits from the noted private reward could exceed the total amount of that reward, $5000, or that the prosecution provided any financial benefit as a result of the Buchanan-Strickland agreement. (See claim 11(E).)
It appears that a copy of the document prepared by Martin memorializing the Strickland-Buchanan agreement was provided to defense counsel Schultz prior to trial and that the billings thereunder, sent to prosecution investigator Martin also were shown to the defense team. (RT 5406.) The state supreme court would not have been unreasonable in finding Schultz’s statement that he “does not recall receiving copies of the bills or being apprised of their existence” (SHCP, Ex. B ¶ 6 at 353) not sufficient evidence otherwise. Significantly, petitioner has not established that Hahus was aware of any particulars of the Strickland-Buchanan relationship apart from the terms in the memorialized document relating to the private reward. (See e.g., RT 5403-07.)
Dismissal of Pending Felony and Misdemeanor Charges against Witness Buchanan
Petitioner revisits allegations raised in claim 11(E) (ineffective assistance of counsel), recast here as a Brady claim that the prosecution failed to disclose an agreement to provide and provided assistance to Buchanan regarding separate felony and misdemeanor charges brought against him around the time of petitioner’s trial. (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶¶ 740-750; Doc. No. 116 at 80:18-87:7; see also claim 11(E).)
Petitioner argues the prosecution failed to disclose the dismissal of felony drug possession counts against Buchanan that arose after he was identified as a witness and prior to petitioner’s trial. (CT 584; SHCP, Ex.’s J, K.) He contends that on July 18, 1989, prior to his trial, “criminal complaint case number 017993-7 ... charging Buchanan with two felony drug possession counts, was dismissed.” (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶ 741; SHCP, Ex. J; see also Supp. CT 8-23, -24, 26-28, 37-39, 51-69.)
Petitioner argues the felony dismissals were part of a plea bargain connected to Buchanan’s testimony in petitioner’s proceeding (see RT 5341-58, 5370-79, 5391-94) and that this deal was meant not to be disclosed (RT 5384; SHCP, Ex. N). He further contends Buchanan told prosecutor Hahus of this deal prior to petitioner’s trial (RT 5380-94) and that Hahus did not believe Buchanan and was unaware of any such deal. (RT 5385-88).
Additionally, petitioner argues 1990 misdemeanor charges were dismissed after. petitioner’s trial by Hahus at least in part in return for Buchanan’s testimony in petitioner’s proceeding. (RT 5396-5403; see also SHCP, Ex. K; Supp. CT. 68-83.) Specifically, he contends that on April 17, 1991, after petitioner’s conviction, Buchanan’s criminal case M48975-7 charging him with a misdemeanor possession of a controlled substance and hypodermic needle was dismissed “in the interests of justice.” (RT 5396-99; see also Doc. No. 51-1 ¶ 741 and n.59, citing Supp. CT 68, 71, 77-78.)
The California Supreme Court considered and rejected these allegations on direct appeal, stating that:
When Ken Hahus, the prosecutor in this case, first spoke to Buchanan after the case was reassigned to him, Buchanan had been released on his own recognizance (OR) in an unrelated narcotics case and had failed to appear. Barbara Dotta, the prosecutor in the narcotics case, had met with defense counsel in chambers, and after defense counsel had pointed out defects in the search warrant, defects which made it impossible to then proceed with the preliminary hearing, and which eventually resulted in dismissal of that case, Ms. Dotta had agreed to Buchanan’s release on OR. However, Buchanan told Mr. Hahus he had been released OR in the narcotics case because he was a prime witness in this case, and that a reporter had been present when counsel met in chambers and reached the purported agreement, but that the reporter had been instructed not to report the agreement, in order to keep defense counsel in this case from learning of it. Mr. Hahus heatedly informed Buchanan no such agreement could have been made because he was the prosecutor in this case, that he would have had to authorize such an agreement, and he had not done so. Mr. Hahus did not inform defense counsel of his conversation with Buchanan until shortly before the hearing on the motion for a new trial. The motion for new trial was based on other grounds. This contention was not added to the motion, and thus the trial court did not address it.
We conclude that while this information would have been favorable to defendant insofar as it tended to impeach Buchanan’s credibility, it was not material because it would have added little to the cumulative impact of the other impeachment evidence.
The jury knew Buchanan was a drug addict, that he had been using drugs almost daily around the time of defendant’s statements, and that he had used drugs the day before his testimony. They knew that, even though he had initially denied it, Buchanan was aware of the $5,000 reward, that Buchanan had told others he wanted the reward for turning defendant in, and that he had then testified the reward was not the reason he had come forward. They knew room and board expenses incurred by Buchanan were to be deducted from the reward. [Citation] They knew Buchanan disliked defendant, and that he wanted revenge against defendant for tearing up a picture of his daughter.
It is not reasonably probable, we conclude, that whatever confidence the jury placed in Buchanan’s testimony would have been fatally undermined by knowing he had made a resoundingly unsuccessful effort to convince Mr. Hahus his testimony entitled him to, in Mr. Ha-hus’s words, “get-out-of-jail-free cards.”9
-FOOTNOTE-
n.9 Defendant contends his trial counsel was ineffective in failing to impeach Buchanan with the fact that the charges in the unrelated narcotics case had been dismissed. This information would have been of no impeachment value. After hearing the motion for a new trial, the trial court concluded the narcotics case “was dismissed because of its weakness months before Mr. Buchanan testified. So there was really no favor done to Mr. Buchanan, and there was no further hook on Mr. Buchanan at that time.”
- END FOOTNOTE
Dickey, 35 Cal.4th at 908-09, 28 Cal.Rptr.3d 647, 111 P.3d 921.
At the outset, the court does not reach respondent’s contention that these allegations are unexhausted (Doc. No. 125 at 146:22-147:3) because these allegations are denied on the merits.
This court is not persuaded the state supreme court’s rejection of these allegations was unreasonable. The district attorneys who were involved in Buchanan’s felony drug case denied any deal by stating charges were dropped because of problems with the search warrant. (RT 5341-58, 5370-79, 5391-94.) The record reflects the felony case was dismissed by the prosecution on July 18, 1989 “due to evidentiary problems” (SHOP, Ex. N-l) and “lack of corpus delicti.” (SHOP, Ex. J-2.)
Petitioner nonetheless argues such a deal to dismiss the felony charges existed, pointing to the time and effort he contends the district attorney’s office devoted to the felony case (Supp. CT 24-25, 29-36, 40-50), and to the guilty plea to one (of three) counts by Buchanan’s co-defendant in the felony case. (Supp. CT 24-25.) However, the state supreme court could reasonably have rejected such surmise by petitioner given the weight of the noted direct testimony. Notably, the trial court rejected the same allegation on motion for new trial, finding the felony case was dismissed for lack of evidence. (RT 1/17/92 at 18-19.)
As for the misdemeanor case, the record reflects the Fresno District Attorney’s Office dismissed the case for “lack of corpus delicti” and “in the interest of justice.” (SHOP, Ex. K-2.) Prosecutor Hahus testified at the new trial motion that it was “hard to say” whether he dismissed the misdemeanor case in reward for Buchanan’s testimony in petitioner’s case. (RT 5402.) Even so, Hahus went on to testify that there was no agreement with Buchanan that the misdemeanor case would be dismissed; and that he never told Buchanan before the latter’s testimony that the case would be dismissed. (RT 5393-5403.) Petitioner has not demonstrated on the facts that Buchanan believed such an agreement to dismiss the misdemeanor charges existed at the time of his testimony. Cf., Giglio, 405 U.S. at 154-55, 92 S.Ct. 763 (evidence of pre-testimony agreement regarding future prosecution of key witness co-conspirator was relevant to credibility of that witness and failure to disclose such evidence was a Brady violation).
For the reasons stated, the state supreme court could reasonably have found that at the time Buchanan testified in petitioner’s proceeding no dismissal deal related to these felony and misdemeanor charges existed with the prosecution and Buchanan did not believe such a deal existed. That court could reasonably have given weight to the direct testimony in the record over petitioner’s surmise and found these allegations not to suggest a reasonable probability of a different result. Schultz’s statement that he was aware of the pending charges and dismissal prior to trial, but did not believe this information would be beneficial to the defense, (SHOP, Ex. B ¶ 7; see also RT 5436), does not suggest otherwise.
Buchanan’s Propensity for Lying
Petitioner alleges that prosecutor Hahus failed to disclose, until just prior to the motion for new trial, his file notes regarding the above discussed alleged felony drug charge plea bargain. (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶¶ 758-768; Doc. No. 116 at 77:2-80:15; SHOP, Ex. N; see also RT 5380-92.) Petitioner argues this failure to disclose these notes during pretrial discovery was prejudicial because this evidence was not otherwise in the record and without it the prosecutor was able to argue in closing that Buchanan testified truthfully. (RT 4969-72.)
However, for the same reasons discussed above the state supreme court reasonably could have found these notes by Hahus of his initial contact with Buchanan not to be materially exculpatory or impeaching. The trial court found Buchanan’s statement regarding the alleged deal was untrue. (RT 1/17/92 at 18-19.) Hahus believed Buchanan’s statement to be untrue (RT 5388), and merely an attempt to parlay benefit in exchange for testimony. (RT 5431-36.) Furthermore, Hahus stated that he did not view Buchanan’s noted statement as a lie and did not see it as favorable to petitioner. (See id.) Instead, Hahus viewed the statement merely as a “flag” showing [Buchanan’s] “relative sophistication in dealing with district attorneys” (RT 5384-88); that the statement simply showed Buchanan was “con-wise.” (RT 4970.)
Accordingly, the state supreme court would not have been unreasonable in finding that even if Hahus had produced notes of Buchanan’s initial contact with him there was no reasonable probability of a different result. As noted, the jury was aware of the facts relating to Buchanan’s credibility. The record before the jury in-eluded other substantial evidence relating to Buchanan’s credibility — including that he was an habitual drug user interested in the reward money and that he held a grudge against petitioner. The jury also was instructed on weighing evidence going toward credibility.
Illegal Drug Activities
Petitioner alleges that prosecutor Hahus failed to disclose a deal with Buchanan not to prosecute the illegal drug activities to which Buchanan testified at trial. (Doc. No. 116 at 70:1-5.) Specifically, petitioner alleges the prosecution failed to pursue criminal charges against Buchanan even though he waived his Fifth Amendment right and admitted under oath at trial that he possessed, used and sold illegal drugs. (See Doc. No. 116 at 87:10-88:15.) Petitioner argues that there must have been an implicit agreement to this effect because no charges were ever filed. (Id.)
As a preliminary matter, the court does not reach respondent’s argument these allegations are unexhausted (Doc. No. 125 at 146:22-147:3) because the allegations are denied on the merits as discussed below.
Petitioner has not made an evidentiary showing that there was an agreement not to prosecute, or that Buchanan believed there was one at the time Buchanan testified at petitioner’s trial. (RT 4737-42.) To the contrary, Buchanan testified prior to these drug related admissions that he was not told by Hahus expressly or implicitly that he (Buchanan) would not be prosecuted. (RT 4741-42, 4754-56.) Nor has Petitioner demonstrated that Buchanan’s waiver of his right against self-incrimination was constitutionally infirm.
Even if there was such a deal, the state supreme court could reasonably have determined any failure to disclose it was not prejudicial. As discussed above, the jury was otherwise aware of Buchanan’s drug related activities and presumable considered such in evaluating his credibility and testimony. Additionally, petitioner has not demonstrated that Buchanan’s drug related admissions were inculpating of petitioner or that absent the admissions petitioner would have realized a different outcome in his proceeding.
Petitioner’s further allegation that Ha-hus failed to disclose such a deal to post-trial defense counsel similarly fails. Petitioner contends that at the hearing on the new trial motion, seven months after trail ended, Hahus was obligated to advise Ms. Hart, who had replaced Schultz as counsel for petitioner, that no prosecution has been initiated regarding the noted admissions. But for the same reasons stated above, petitioner has not demonstrated such a deal existed or if it did that he was prejudiced by it.
(Hi) Cumulative Effect of Napue and Brady Errors
Petitioner finally alleges that the state supreme court was unreasonable in finding no cumulative material violation under Na-pue and Brady. (See Doc. No. 116 at 28:4-99:7.)
“The Supreme Court has clearly established that the combined effect of multiple trial errors may give rise to a due process violation if it renders a trial fundamentally unfair, even where each error considered individually would not require reversal.” Parle v. Runnels, 505 F.3d 922, 928 (9th Cir. 2007), citing Donnelly, 416 U.S. at 643, 94 S.Ct. 1868.
Although individual errors looked at separately may not rise to the level of reversible error, their cumulative effect may nevertheless be so prejudicial as to require reversal. Necoechea, 986 F.2d at 1282 (“In reviewing for cumulative error, the court must review all errors preserved for appeal and all plain error.”). However, the fact that errors have been committed during a trial does not mean that reversal is required. “[WJhile a defendant is entitled to a fair trial; [she] is not entitled to a perfect trial, for there are no perfect trials.” United States v. Payne, 944 F.2d 1458, 1477 (9th Cir. 1991).
Here, petitioner argues the state supreme court ignored facts that “(1) Buchanan was continuing to rely on the $5,000 reward, not on earnings from property management and maintenance, to pay his debt to the landlady; (2) [Buchanan] was beholden to the state for nailing down the agreement that allowed him to obtain room and board on credit; (3) [Hahus] met with [Buchanan] at least a dozen times prior to the trial; and (4) [Buchanan] had no real concern about being prosecuted— let alone facing the death penalty — for perjury.” (Doc. No. 116 at 61:22-62:1.)
Petitioner argues that confidence in the verdict is undermined by: “1) the jury being deprived of knowing that Buchanan lied to the DA to gain personal advantage with respect to his willingness to testify in this case; 2) the DA’s arguments to the jury that Buchanan was honest and reliable; and 3) the propensity of most all juries to disregard the testimony of liars, as they are instructed they can do.” (Doc. No. 128 at 84:7-14.) He argues that absent these errors prosecutor Hahus would have had to present weaker theories, present less evidence, and refrain from making numerous arguments that vouched for Buchanan’s veracity. This, petitioner argues would have “fueled a withering cross-examination, destroying confidence in Buchanan’s story and raising a substantial implication that the DA had coached him during their dozen or more meetings.” (Id. at 83:14-18.)
Cumulative prejudice arising from concurrent Brady and Napue error is analyzed as follows:
[W]e first consider the Napue violations collectively and ask whether there is “any reasonable likelihood that the false testimony could have affected the judgment of the jury.” If so, habeas relief must be granted. However, if the Napue errors are not material standing alone, we consider all of the Napue and Brady violations collectively and ask whether “there is a reasonable probability that, but for counsel’s unprofessional errors, the result of the proceeding would have been different.” At both stages, we must ask whether the defendant “received ... a trial resulting in a verdict worthy of confidence.”
Jackson v. Brown, 513 F.3d 1057, 1076 (9th Cir. 2008).
As a preliminary matter, the court is unpersuaded by respondent’s argument based upon Imbler v. Pachtman, that a claim under Brady can be analyzed under the Napue standard. See 424 U.S. 409, 431 n.34, 96 S.Ct. 984, 47 L.Ed.2d 128 (1976). The Imbler court’s holding relates to pros-ecutorial immunity in the context of Brady and Napue and does not support respondent’s asserted standard of review for cumulative error under Napue and Brady.
For the reasons discussed above, the state supreme court could reasonably have found no individual and cumulative prejudice under Napue and Brady. First, considering only the Napue allegations, that court could have found no reasonable likelihood that the false testimony could have affected the judgment of the jury. For the reasons stated above, the state supreme court could reasonably have rejected petitioner’s allegations regarding the memori-alization of the Strickland agreement; Stokes’s testimony of Goldman’s comments to him; and the court’s notice of Penal Code section 128. Nor does it appear the alleged false testimony materially impacted the ability of the jury to evaluate Buchanan’s credibility given the noted evidence that was otherwise before the jury.
Second, even when combined with the alleged Brady violations, there is no reasonable probability that the result of the proceeding would have been different. For the reasons stated above, the state supreme court could reasonably have rejected allegations regarding prejudicial undisclosed contacts between Buchanan and Hahus including relating to alleged lies by Buchanan; the Strickland agreement; dismissal of criminal charges; and illegal drug usé by Buchanan. Cf., Horton v. Mayle, 408 F.3d 570, 578-79 (9th Cir. 2005) (holding that failing to disclose a leniency deal was material because the witness’s testimony was “central to the prosecution’s case” and “the deal would have provided powerful and unique impeachment evidence demonstrating that [the witness] had an interest in fabricating his testimony”).
There was no cumulative error under Napue and Brady because there was no individual constitutional error. See Thompson, 86 F.3d at 1521. For the reasons stated, the state supreme court could reasonably have found the alleged Napue and Brady errors not sufficient to “undermine confidence in the outcome” of the trial. See Bagley, 473 U.S. at 682, 105 S.Ct. 3375.
(iv) Conclusions
For the reasons stated, the state supreme court could reasonably have found petitioner failed to demonstrate prosecuto-rial misconduct under Brady and Napue.
Accordingly, the California Supreme Court’s rejection of this claim was not contrary to, or an unreasonable application of, clearly established federal law, or an unreasonable determination of the facts in light of the evidence presented in the state court proceeding. 28 U.S.C. § 2254(d).
Claim XIII is denied.
3. Review of Claims XIV and XV
Petitioner alleges prosecutorial misconduct by failing to collect and preserve and/or making unavailable and destroying material potentially exculpatory evidence, denying his rights under the Fifth, Sixth, Eighth and Fourteenth Amendments of the United States Constitution. (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶¶ 769-825.)
a. Clearly Established Law
(i) Prosecutorial Misconduct
The clearly established law for prosecu-torial misconduct is set out in claim XIII, ante.
(ii) Potentially Exculpatory Evidence
The failure of a state to preserve evidence “of which no more can be said than it could have been subjected to tests, the results of which might have exonerated the defendant,” is not a denial of due process of the law “unless a criminal defendant can show bad faith on the part of the police.” Youngblood, 488 U.S. at 57, 109 S.Ct. 333; Mitchell, 878 F.2d at 321.
Where the government fails to preserve evidence that is only potentially exculpatory, the right to due process is violated only if [the evidence] possesses “an exculpatory value that was apparent before the evidence was destroyed, and [is] of such a nature that the defendant would be unable to obtain comparable evidence by other reasonably available means.” Trombetta, 467 U.S. at 489, 104 S.Ct. 2528. The defendant in Youngblood failed to establish a due process violation because the evidence was only “potentially useful” and the actions of the police could “at worst be described as negligent.” Youngblood, 488 U.S. at 58, 109 S.Ct. 333.
b. Analysis of Claim XIV
Petitioner alleges that the prosecution, in knowing bad faith failed to collect and preserve and/or destroyed material physical and biologic exculpating evidence.
(i) State Court Direct and Collateral Review
The California Supreme Court summarily denied these allegations on the merits and denied certain allegations as procedurally barred, on habeas review. (Lod. Doc. No. 31.)
(ii) Failure to Collect and Preserve Physical Evidence
Petitioner alleges the state failed to collect and preserve material physical evidence at the crime scene and elsewhere.
Crime Scene Fingerprint Evidence
Petitioner argues that by virtue of the law enforcement’s bad faith failure to conduct a reasonable crime scene examination, no fingerprints from anyone were found at the crime scene notwithstanding the two victims and five witnesses known to have been in the home during the period from the murders until the police examination of the crime scene. (Doc. No. 116 at 121:1-24; RT 4214-33.) He suggests that such fingerprint evidence would have been exculpatory.
Petitioner argues the police failed to collect and examine two disconnected telephones with blood on them (RT 4321, 4330-31) found placed together in the living room. (RT 4320-21; SSHCP, Ex. 9 at 322.) He surmises bad faith was involved because i) RC was known to have a fascination with telephones (RT 4320-22) and ii) detective Stokes believed more than one perpetrator was likely involved in the crimes. (RT 4628-29.) He cites Miller v. Vasquez and argues this alleged bad faith failure to collect and preserve potentially exculpatory evidence violated his due process rights. 868 F.2d 1116, 1120-21 (9th Cir. 1989) (Youngblood applies where the state in bad faith fails to collect potentially exculpatory evidence); (see also Doc. No. 51-1- at 233:9-12).
However, the state supreme court reasonably could have rejected these allegations. The record reflects police “I” Bureau officers dusted the crime scene including all areas likely to yield usable latent prints; they simply did not find any usable prints to collect. (RT 4211-33; see also Doc. No. 125 at 149:15-17.) The Venetian blinds and window coverings at the crime scene were checked and found not to be missing any cord. (RT 4326-28.) Petitioner has not shown the existence of potentially useful fingerprints or other physical evidence that law enforcement failed to recover from the crime scene in bad faith. See e.g., United States v. Burdeau, 168 F.3d 352, 356-57 (9th Cir. 1999) (there are many reasons not to find usable prints).
The state supreme court reasonably could have found these allegations to be only speculative.
Easy 8 Motel
Petitioner argues that by virtue of the law enforcement’s bad faith failure to examine the Fresno hotel room rented by RC shortly after the murders (RT 4420-34), potentially exculpatory evidence was lost including possible forensic evidence linking Buchanan to the crimes. (Doc. No. 116 at 123:6-124:15.) He points out that even though RC left his belongings including an empty prescription drug vial belonging to Buchanan at the Easy 8 Motel during his flight after the murders (RT 4541-42), the police did not search for Buchanan or investigate Buchanan’s potential involvement in the crimes. All this, he surmises, deprived him of potentially exculpating evidence.
But the state supreme court could have determined the police investigation of the room was reasonable. Petitioner infers from the prescription drug vial and the fact Buchanan had access to a car that Buchanan was in the room with RC. Yet the weight of the evidentiary record seems not to support such an inference. The desk clerk at the Easy 8 Motel, Mr. Yeon, testified at trial that RC registered alone; Yeon makes no mention of anyone accompanying RC while at the Motel. (RT 4420-34.) Yeon testified he was interviewed by detective Stokes shortly after RC departed the Motel and that the police collected RC’s registration card, entered his room and collected RC’s remaining belongings. (Id.) Detective Stokes’s testimony on these events tracked Yeon’s; Stokes also testified that at the time, police were unfamiliar with Buchanan. (RT 4540-42.)
Additionally, petitioner’s allegation that Buchanan was RC’s accomplice in the crimes fails for the reasons discussed in claim 11(B).
Accordingly, petitioner has not pointed to facts in the record reasonably suggesting potentially exculpatory evidence at the Easy 8 Motel that was not recovered by law enforcement in bad faith.
Harvard Avenue Apartment
Petitioner argues that law enforcement’s bad faith failure to examine the Harvard Avenue apartment RC shared with petitioner and others until three months after the murders resulted in the loss of potentially exculpatory evidence. (Doc. No. 116 at 128:6-131:5.) He argues that although law enforcement almost immediately suspected RC and one or more accomplices in the murders, (RT 4628), no warrant was sought to search the apartment for crime scene property or evidence linked to the crimes, such as the bloody knife and the ligature found at the crime scene.
However, petitioner has not demonstrated on the evidentiary record that any such potentially exculpating property or evidence existed and was lost. He argues law enforcement may have allowed the Venetian blind recovered by detective Stokes, from which only petitioner’s fingerprint was lifted, to be contaminated. (See claims 11(F), II(S).) He suggests this was because of detective Stokes’s requested that landlord, Ms. Hays, handle the blind. (Id.) These same allegations were discussed above in claims 11(F), II(S) and fail for the reasons stated therein. Notably, petitioner’s own expert was unable to identify on the Venetian blind any additional usable latent prints suitable for comparison. (Id.; see also Doc. No. 51-1 ¶ 786.)
Petitioner argues law enforcement similarly delayed several months investigating the ligature cord used in the murder of Freiri; that it was not until three months after the murders that Stokes returned to the then vacated and cleaned apartment to try and match the cord to a Venetian blind. (RT 4514-30.) Petitioner argues that the Venetian blind recovered by Stokes from Hays had no cord remaining and had been handled and possibly altered by Hays. (See Id.) Petitioner argues that Stokes, without foundation, took and had analyzed cord samples from the other three blinds remaining in the apartment even though those blinds were not missing any cord.
However, as noted in claims 11(F) and II(S), the jury was informed that the crime scene ligature was not a match for the samples taken by detective Stokes. For the reasons stated in claims 11(F), II(S), the state supreme court could reasonably have rejected these same allegations couched here as prosecutorial error. Moreover, petitioner has not pointed to facts in the record suggesting Stokes or the prosecution intentionally delayed recovery of potentially exculpatory evidence or otherwise acted in bad faith in such regards. See Trombetta, 467 U.S. at 489, 104 S.Ct. 2528. Instead it appears that Stokes’s forensic investigation of the Harvard Avenue apartment took place once petitioner was identified by Buchanan as a suspect.
(in) Failure to Collect and Preserve Biologic Evidence
Petitioner revisits allegation in claims 11(B), 11(C), 11(D) above, here couched as claimed prosecutorial error. (Doc. No. 51-1 ¶¶ 807-813.) He complains of the prosecution’s bad faith failure to collect and preserve crime scene biologic evidence. (Doc. No. 116 at 134:22-145:20.) Specifically, he argues fingernail scrapings, saliva, debris from a wound on Mr. Freiri’s head, blood evidence and hair found at the crime scene were not properly preserved for analysis and that some of this evidence was lost at the state crime lab for up to 5 months. He notes in support that state criminologist Cortner testified at trial that most of blood evidence could not be analyzed. (RT 4567-69.)
However, the state supreme court reasonably could have rejected these allegations. As discussed in claims 11(B), 11(C), 11(D), the biologic evidence recovered and analyzed by law enforcement was not inculpating of petitioner. This allowed Schultz to argue at trial the primary defense that RC was the sole perpetrator. (RT 4985-87.) Fingernail scrapings tested positive for the presence of blood, but that the samples were insufficient for further analysis, i.e., petitioner was not a match. A hair found on the hand of victim Freiri was not a match for petitioner; defense forensic expert Barnett’s pre-trial analysis confirmed as much. Of the seven blood swabs taken, three tested positive for blood, but only two could be typed and those excluded petitioner as a possible source. Petitioner has not demonstrated that the blood swabs which returned inconclusive results and the unanalyzed wound debris recovered from the scalp of victim Freiri, were testable when collected.
Additionally, the state supreme court could reasonably have found any failure to preserve potentially useful biologic evidence resulted from mere negligence and as such not a violation of petitioner’s constitutional rights. See e.g., Youngblood, 488 U.S. at 58, 109 S.Ct. 333 (“The failure of the police to refrigerate the clothing and to perform tests on the semen samples can at worst be described as negligent.”). Petitioner has not pointed to facts in the state record suggesting Stokes or the prosecution intentionally failed to recover and preserve potentially exculpating evidence or otherwise acted in bad faith in these regards. See Trombetta, 467 U.S. at 489, 104 S.Ct. 2528.
